## Where sources disagree — and how to resolve it

- **Which typeface carries the brand. Against defaults: Inter, Roboto and system fonts must not be the brand voice (ai_tells.0.2, 13/13); pick a distinctive, uncommon face (typography.0.2, 10/13); use a licensed or custom face (3/3); name these defaults to the AI as things to avoid (3/4). For defaults: set the whole UI in one clean sans such as SF/system-ui, Inter, DM Sans, Plus Jakarta or Geist (typography.0.5, 9/11); prefer the system font for its optical sizing (5/5); choose timeless families, not trendy ones (4/4); default to system text styles (4/5). On top of that, 'leave nothing at default' (5/5).** — Decide by layer and by surface. ai_tells.0.2 already allows a neutral workhorse under the brand voice. DEFAULT (marketing site, brand surfaces, web product): headings and brand moments use a licensed face with character from a serious foundry. It should be unusual in its category but classic in structure: a well-drawn grotesk or humanist with optical sizes, or a commissioned cut. It should not be a novelty face or one of the current AI choices (Inter, Geist, DM Sans, Plus Jakarta or Space Grotesk as display). Body and UI can use the same family's Text cut, which is Apple's own method (one proprietary family with Display/Text optical sizes), or a neutral workhorse. Read 'uncommon' as 'specific to this brand', not 'new this year'; that settles the clash with typography.0.7. Try 3 candidates in the real layout. typography.0.5 WINS OUTRIGHT in native apps: on iOS/macOS use SF through system text styles, which give Dynamic Type, optical sizes and per-size tracking for free. Choosing SF there is a deliberate decision, so process_mindset.0.34 is satisfied. Keep the brand face to a few display moments (onboarding, large titles, empty states). It also wins for dense data tools, where one neutral sans with tabular figures is right. Licensing note: SF Pro may not be self-hosted for a non-Apple website, and a system-ui stack renders SF only on Apple devices (Segoe or Roboto elsewhere). A web brand that wants the same voice everywhere therefore needs its own licensed face.
- **Never use pure #000/#FFF (color.0.28, 6/6), and don't leave the page on a flat pure-white canvas (3/3). Against that: shoot products on a seamless white or #F5F5F7 sweep (7/7); use pure-white cards on an off-white page (3/3); give premium product pages a pure-black canvas (4/4); white background with near-black text (5/5); increased-contrast mode uses #000 on #fff (3/4).** — Treat ink and surface as separate questions. INK (wins by default): never pure #000 text on #FFF. Use a near-black around #1d1d1f to #111, and on dark grounds cap text at about 95% lightness. SURFACES: pure #FFFFFF is fine when it is not the only surface. Pair it with an off-white/light-grey tier around #F5F5F7, either for alternating sections or as the raised card on an off-white page. The AI tell is one flat white canvas with nothing else. Match each product-photo sweep exactly to its section surface so cut-outs sit seamlessly. Pure #000 is correct only for (a) dark product-render sections whose renders were lit on black, (b) true-black media/video viewers on OLED, and (c) the prefers-contrast: more mode. Every other dark ground is off-black (#0a0a0a to #121212, or brand-tinted).
- **For gradients: give backgrounds atmosphere with layered gradients, patterns, textures and noise rather than flat colour (4/4); add grain (3/3); prompt for atmospheric backgrounds (3/4). Against: no purple/blue gradients (11/11); no decorative mesh, aurora or glow backgrounds (3/3); don't stack effects (7/7); use real photography rather than gradients or glows (5/5); show the real product rather than orbs (6/6); drop gradients when simplifying (3/3).** — The avoid side wins by default. It has roughly 30 creators to roughly 11, and gradient glows are the most-cited AI fingerprint. Atmosphere should come from real things: photography, lit product renders, real material texture, or a flat tinted ground. A gradient is allowed only when something physical motivates it: light falling across a surface, a vignette that seats a product, or a ramp built from shades of the existing brand hue (color.0.23). Use it at hero or section scale, keep contrast low, and never put it on buttons or text or as a glow at top centre. Use grain only at a few percent opacity on photo or flat-colour fields, to stop banding or add material warmth, and leave out any section meant to look crisp. Purple-to-blue/cyan, mesh and aurora: never, unless purple is an existing brand asset, and even then never as a gradient on white.
- **Animate only where motion has a job you can state (11/11). Cut ambient decoration: marquees, cursor-chasing glows, image hover zoom, pulsing dots (3/3). Don't stack effects (7/7); keep motion subtle (3/3). Against: never ship a static page, and give marketing pages 2-3 motions including a hero entrance and a scroll-linked effect (4/4); animate every state change (4/4); scrub a product sequence with scroll (4/4); loop an ambient video behind the hero (3/3).** — motion_interaction.0.0 governs: every motion must pass the one-sentence job test. State-change animation (motion_interaction.0.19) passes automatically because it gives feedback and continuity, so product UI should animate state changes at 150-300ms. A marketing page gets ONE signature story motion, such as a scroll-scrubbed product reveal or a sticky depth section. Use it only when there is a real product or object to reveal and that motion is the page's chosen main idea. Add a short entrance (600ms or less, never delaying content or LCP) and real hover and press states. That meets the 'not static' minimum without ornament. A looping hero video is allowed only when the footage IS the content (a place, the product in use): muted, with a poster frame and a pause control, never generic b-roll. Cut all decorative ambient motion: marquees, cursor followers, meteors, parallax on every section, image hover zoom. Everything gets a prefers-reduced-motion path (swap to fades or static) and a low-power fallback.
- **Avoid trends: build on fundamentals rather than yearly fashion (3/3); avoid fashionable styling (3/3); use timeless faces (4/4); the fashionable 'Linear look' reads as AI (4/4); don't ship a new skin for novelty (6/6). Follow them: choose a still-uncommon typeface (10/13); adopt translucency and specular materials, Apple's current Liquid Glass direction (3/3, 4/4); move into 'unclaimed' palette territory (4/4); anchor the look in a genre world such as solarpunk or RPG fantasy (4/5).** — Timelessness wins by default. Adopt a trend only when it does a job or the platform supplies it. For example, use Liquid Glass through native system components on Apple platforms: at that point it is convention, not fashion. 'Uncommon' type and palette should mean specific to the brand's own world, not new this season. Choose well-made, structurally classic forms and use them in an unexpected context. A genre or 'named world' look applies only when it is the product's own world (a game, a publication, a scientific tool), not a costume. Test each candidate: would it still look deliberate in 5 years, and would you choose it if no competitor were doing it? Chasing unclaimed territory for its own sake is trend-following in reverse.
- **Dark muted tones and black canvases signal premium (4/4), and statement or pricing sections can flip to dark (5/5). Against: choose light or dark from the real use scene, and use dark only when it says something true (4/4); dark mode plus gradients plus glass is the fashionable AI look (4/4); no stock dark theme (3/3); purple glow on near-black is the template (4/4). Also: every token should resolve per appearance and follow the OS (3/4).** — Dark is not a shortcut to premium. DEFAULT is light, which is also how Apple's own marketing works: mostly white and light grey with near-black ink. Go dark only when the use scene argues for it: pro or cinematic product renders, photo/video/media tools, developer tools, night-use apps, space or automotive subjects. The best use of color.0.10 and color.0.54 is one inverted section per page for rhythm or a product-render moment, not a whole dark theme. When dark, use tinted off-black, show elevation with progressively lighter surfaces, and use no purple glow and no glass stack. Product apps and tools must support both appearances through tokens and follow the OS (color.0.15). Marketing pages design one deliberate appearance instead of auto-inverting art-directed imagery.
- **Use far more whitespace than feels necessary, 2-3x defaults (16/17), and give the key element extra space (3/3). Against: choose compact density for dashboards and directories (3/3); keep information density and don't mistake minimal for simple (4/4); trend minimalism (lots of space, small headline, tiny grey text) looks generic (3/3); stats spread across too much empty space look fake (3/3).** — Generous whitespace wins by default on marketing, editorial, product-story and hero layouts. It has the strongest evidence in the set and matches Apple (desktop section padding of roughly 80-160px). But whitespace reads as premium only alongside strong hierarchy: large confident type, a real image or product, full-contrast primary text. If the result is small grey type floating in emptiness, ai_tells.0.43 applies: raise the type scale and contrast rather than adding elements. For tools, dashboards, tables, directories, settings and checkout, spacing_layout.0.50 wins. Choose density deliberately from the lower steps of the same scale (4/8/12/16). Never get there by deleting the relationship-based gaps, and never by hiding functions (hierarchy.0.30). The rule that gaps follow relationships (spacing_layout.0.2) holds in both modes; only the sizes change.
- **For removal: cut until nothing more can go, and take one more thing away (8/9); every element must earn its place (8/9); delete decoration that carries no information (5/5). For character: every design needs a point of view and aesthetic risk, and removing defects is only the floor (4/5); don't ship an all-grey UI (3/3); stripped minimalism has no character (3/3); repeat a brand motif across the site (4/4); don't default to minimal and sleek when the brand calls for warmth (8/8).** — Remove ornament, keep character. The removal pass wins by default for anything that is decoration without information: dots, pills, stamps, rotated text, icon rows, stat strips, extra borders. Character then comes from ONE deliberately chosen main move (process_mindset.0.14), carried by elements that already do work: the typeface, a committed colour, art-directed imagery, scale, or one signature motion. It does not come from added ornament. A brand motif earns its place only where it also does a job (primary button, link arrow, loader, image mask) and appears in no more than 3-4 places. 'Apple-calibre' means discipline and craft, not a fixed minimal look; warmth can be expressed with the same restraint. Final test: after the removal pass, run the swap test (brand_identity.0.23). If it fails because the page reads as a generic grey template, commit harder on an existing element (bigger type, real colour, better image) instead of adding new ones.
- **Restrained colour: neutrals plus exactly one accent (9/9); accent only on CTAs and selected states (8/8); chrome in black, white and grey (7/8); one accent per page (4/4). Committed colour: let one hue own 30-60% of the surface (4/4); no all-grey UI (3/3); a saturated hue as the ground (4/4); 2-3 colours (5/5). Further tension: keep chroma at or below about 0.15-0.2 (4/4); premium uses muted tones, not bright dominants (4/4).** — Restraint wins by default and almost always in product UI. That means tinted neutrals, one accent reserved for action, selection and focus, and semantic state colours. Chart palettes are a separate data-scale token set, not extra accents. Committed colour applies at SECTION or CAMPAIGN scale on marketing and brand surfaces when the colour is itself content: a product colourway (Apple floods sections in an iPhone or iMac colour), a campaign, or a brand whose identity is a colour. Then one hue owns that section, with type in white or tints of the same hue (color.0.24). The accent stays the only interactive colour, and chroma stays moderate unless the product itself is that saturated. A second or third brand colour (color.0.4) needs a named job such as illustration or data. Never give each card, stat or icon chip its own hue (ai_tells.0.29).
- **Replace the purple AI look with warm cream, beige, earth tones and terracotta (color.0.36, 4/4). Against: cream with rust/terracotta and a serif is already spent (4/4); avoid the vendor's beige, off-red and big-serif house style (3/3). Separately: pick a seed colour you like and build the rest from colour-wheel harmony (4/4), versus taking the palette from a concrete source in the subject's world (4/4) and choosing hue for its meaning in the domain (3/3).** — Purple-on-dark (11/11) and the cream-terracotta-serif trio are now both AI defaults, so swapping one for the other fixes nothing. DEFAULT: derive the palette from the subject's own world (brand guidelines, the product's materials, photography of the real place or product, the domain's conventions) and name tones after it (color.0.34, process_mindset.0.13). Colour-wheel harmony is only a tool for generating supporting hues from that sourced seed, never the source itself, and 'a colour I like' is not a reason. Cream or warm grounds are valid only when the subject really is made of them (paper, bakery, ceramics, hospitality). Even then, break the trio: pair cream with a non-terracotta accent and a sans display. Purple is valid only as an existing brand asset.
- **Tint neutrals and dark grounds toward the brand hue (6/7), and don't leave a cool grey-white canvas (3/3). Against: build greys as a zero-saturation ramp like iOS system greys (3/3). Both share the rule of locking one neutral temperature (5/5).** — DEFAULT: a barely tinted ramp (OKLCH chroma about 0.005-0.02, hue taken from the accent, one temperature), which ties the palette together without anyone noticing. Use true neutral or Apple-like slightly cool greys when content colour must be judged accurately (product colourways, photography, fashion, colour tools, data-viz backgrounds) or when the brand is deliberately cold and technical. Either way: one temperature across the whole product, steps differing only in lightness, one role per step. On coloured grounds, shift shadows, borders and secondary text toward that ground's hue (color.0.32).
- **Build depth with layered translucency, blur, parallax and specular light (3/3); make controls bend, refract and catch highlights so every interaction feels 'fidget-worthy' (4/4). Against: glassmorphism as the main visual is an AI tell (5/5); don't ship a new material skin for novelty (6/6); don't pile saturated colour onto glass (4/4); serve solid surfaces on low-power devices (3/3).** — Glass belongs only to the functional layer that floats over content: sticky nav and sub-nav, toolbars, tab bars, sheets, overlays. components_ui.0.31 and ai_tells.0.25 both agree on this. There it replaces a shadow, with a solid fallback for reduced transparency and low power. Content (cards, panels, hero) stays solid. Physically responsive materials (refraction, specular highlights, bend) apply only on native Apple platforms, where the OS supplies them through system components. Don't fake them by hand on the web or in cross-platform UI: there they read as imitation and cost performance. Never put saturated colour behind heavy blur.
- **Use spring physics with a slight overshoot so elements carry momentum (4/4), and make controls feel lively (4/4). Against: default to a strong ease-out (4/4); keep motion subtle and never flashy (3/3); keep it short, capped around 300ms (3/3); build CSS-first (4/4).** — DEFAULT for UI transitions: interruptible CSS transitions with a strong ease-out such as cubic-bezier(0.16,1,0.3,1), 150-300ms, exits faster than entrances, no overshoot. Use springs where the motion is driven by direct manipulation or carries real momentum: drag, swipe, sheet or drawer release, toggles, reorder, pinch. They are also the native default in SwiftUI. Keep bounce low: about 0-0.15, critically damped in productivity UI, more only for light, playful objects in consumer or brand moments. Springs also meet the interruptibility rule (motion_interaction.0.40). Approximate springs with CSS linear() easing before reaching for a JS library.
- **One sans family, no second display face (9/11). Against: pair contrasting classifications for headings and body (7/8); use an editorial display serif for headlines (4/4). Allowed count: at most two faces, three as a ceiling (6/6).** — DEFAULT: one family with optical sizes and 2-3 weights, the Apple model (SF nearly everywhere, New York rarely). Add a second face only when it has a named job the first can't do: (a) an editorial, literary, luxury or heritage brand whose voice is a serif display; or (b) monospace for code and data (typography.0.20), which doesn't count as a brand voice. When pairing, follow typography.0.14: different classifications, shared mood, never two near-identical sans. Ceiling: two voices plus mono. If a second face is there only to look 'designed', cut it.
- **Set headlines in a large editorial display serif, switching to a Didone for premium (4/4). Against: an oversized, often italic display serif in the hero is an AI default (3/3); the cream + terracotta + serif look is spent (4/4); avoid the vendor's big-serif house style (3/3).** — A sans display wins by default for modern tech and product work, consistent with Apple calibre. A serif display applies only when the brand or brief is genuinely editorial, literary, hospitality, fashion/luxury or heritage. Even then: upright rather than italic; not from the overused pool (Fraunces, Instrument Serif, Playfair, Recoleta, Newsreader italic); not on a cream ground with a terracotta accent; and used throughout, not as an italic accent word. A high-contrast Didone suits fashion and luxury only at large sizes (roughly 32px and up, where its hairlines survive). Inside product UI, a serif reserved for a few emotional beats (welcome, success) is an acceptable narrow role.
- **Give monospace narrow jobs, including image captions and small technical eyebrows (5/5). Against: mono used as a 'technical' costume on labels and subheads is a tell (3/3); the tiny tracked mono eyebrow (about 10.5px, 0.22em) is the signature tell (7/7).** — The ai_tells side wins for eyebrows and labels. Use mono only for content that is literally code-like or must line up in character cells: code, CLI output, IDs and hashes, timestamps, tabular data, specs. Don't use it for eyebrows, section labels or marketing captions, even on developer products. If the need is only aligned numbers, use tabular figures in the main face (typography.0.62) instead of mono.
- **Full-bleed image hero with a centred headline overlaid (4/4); centre the main focal element (3/3). Against: split the hero into a ~40% text column and an image, and never let the image sit behind text (3/3). Meanwhile the centred SaaS hero and the copy-left/image-right split are both banned as templates (4/4), and display type should cross behind the product (5/5). Also: centre only short display text and keep one shared left edge (4/4).** — Let the star decide the structure. For a physical product or place with strong imagery: a centred, product-led hero (product large on the vertical axis, headline of 2 lines or fewer, one primary CTA plus a text link), or a full-bleed image with overlaid text. Overlay is allowed only on an art-directed calm area with verified contrast or a subtle scrim (accessibility_performance.0.3). For software or services, where the explanation matters more than the picture: a left-aligned text stack on the page's shared edge, with a real product UI visual. A split layout is fine as long as it drops the template parts (badge row, stat row, ghost 'Watch demo' button). ai_tells.0.36 bans that bundle, not centring or splitting as such. Type overlapping the product is the signature move for one hero per site, not a default. Keep stats, logos and pill eyebrows out of the first viewport (hierarchy.0.40). Paragraphs are never centred.
- **Oversized display type as the hero's main visual: near viewport width, cropped, rotated about 30 degrees, crossing the product (5/5). Against: the subject or product image should be the largest thing on the canvas (4/4, 3/3); one focal point and at most 2 prominent elements (8/8); keep logos and wordmarks small (3/3); delete rotated text (5/5); never enlarge text to fill empty space (16/17).** — One star per view. If the product or subject is visually strong, it is the largest element and type supports it. Type becomes the star when there is no strong visual (services, abstract software, a name reveal or launch). It can also form one combined focal unit when a product name at wordmark scale sits behind the product. Make type huge because it is the subject, never to fill space. Rotated type belongs only in campaign or poster contexts with a reason; cut it on product pages. The logo in the site chrome stays small; a giant wordmark is a hero composition, not the nav logo.
- **Drop eyebrow labels and pill badges above headings, at most one per three sections (7/7); no all-caps tracked labels by default (4/4). Against: track uppercase eyebrows at +0.03 to +0.1em (11/11, which assumes eyebrows exist); all-caps is fine for short labels such as eyebrows (4/4); a hero can include an eyebrow (3/3).** — DEFAULT: no eyebrow; move its words into the heading. An eyebrow survives only when it carries information the headline doesn't. Examples: the product name above a short tagline (Apple's pattern), a category on an editorial article, a date on a news item. Then use at most about one per three sections, in sentence or title case at a readable size. If caps are used for short tags, apply typography.0.51's modest +0.03 to +0.1em. The 0.18-0.22em tracked tiny eyebrow is the tell. typography.0.51's tracking rules for headings and body stand untouched.
- **No grid of rounded icon + title + promise cards or uniform bento (7/7); don't wrap every block in a card (4/4); separate with spacing first (4/4, 5/5). Against: show elevation with tonal card surfaces (3/3); break dense content into self-contained modules (5/5); design cards once and reuse them (4/5).** — By default, spacing and type do the separating. A container appears only when the content is an object (product, file, plan, message, item), when the module is interactive, or when it needs its own surface to hold real media. Feature sections are built from real product visuals, not icon tiles. An Apple-style bento is acceptable only when every tile holds a distinct real visual or a real measured fact, and tile sizes vary by importance; never a uniform 3x2 of icon, bold title and one-line promise. Where cards are justified: tonal elevation (white on off-white), one reused card component, no nesting.
- **Don't assemble the category's stock section sequence: logo cloud, stat strip, testimonial cards, three-tier pricing and so on (14/14). No big-number template or vanity metrics (3/3); no invented stats (3/3); cut stat strips (8/9). Against: show concrete social proof such as named testimonials, logo walls, hard numbers and founder photos (3/3), with real SVG logos (3/3).** — Keep trust signals that are real and needed; drop the template form. DEFAULT: no section exists just because the category usually has one. Each must answer a question the visitor actually has. Proof must be verifiable: named customers with permission, real SVG logos, measured numbers with their context. Place it next to the claim it supports (a battery figure inside the battery section), not in a hero stat strip. Render it in the page's own system; one strong quote with a real photo beats a row of three cards. Logo walls suit B2B, where buyers look for their peers. Founder and team proof suits services and small brands. Product pages lean on product facts. Highlight a pricing tier only if it really is the recommended one.
- **Follow conventions (Jakob's law) (8/8); default to system and design-system components (4/5); keep product chrome a little boring (4/4); study how shipped products solve each pattern (4/4). Against: take aesthetic risks and break expected patterns (4/5); make every section specific enough to pass a swap test (4/4); a style guessable from the category is a tell (4/4); mine history instead of current web conventions (3/3).** — Decide by layer. Interaction, information architecture and controls stay CONVENTIONAL by default: logo top-left, standard nav, forms, checkout, modals, system components, platform rules (HIG on Apple). Visual voice, imagery, typography, composition and copy are where distinctiveness and risk belong. Break an interaction convention only when a new interaction is the product's core value; then label it and test it with users (process_mindset.0.44). In product UI the chrome stays in the background, and personality lives in content, empty states, onboarding and a few signature moments. On marketing surfaces every section must pass the swap test. Use live-product libraries to learn how patterns work, and history, print and the domain to source the look.
- **Start from a proven base: a screenshot, template, imported site or someone else's DESIGN.md (7/7). Design with a coded component library (5/5) and in live code rather than frozen mockups (4/4). Against: don't assemble from templates (14/14); don't ship library defaults (4/4); work top-down from an approved greyscale wireframe, styling last (7/7); mock up the look before writing code (3/3); explore 3+ radically different directions (6/6); greyscale first (3/3) yet define exact visual values before layout (5/5).** — Use this sequence. (1) Brief, one-line thesis and real content. (2) Decide the colour and type strategy early and write the tokens into DESIGN.md. Decide early, apply late: that reconciles brand_identity.0.7 with color.0.42. (3) Greyscale structure with real copy. (4) Explore 3 or more visual directions as quick static frames or throwaway code, whichever is cheaper. Use static frames when starting in code would anchor you to the library's look. (5) Converge, then build in real code with a component library as the engine, restyling every token, font, icon and radius. (6) Tune values live in the browser and run QA. Mine references and templates only for structure and spacing ratios, never for visual identity, which settles the clash with ai_tells.0.41. Nothing ships from a one-shot AI pass (9/9, 7/8).
- **Treat a redesign as evolution: keep placements, nav labels and URLs, and use low-risk levers first (6/7); protect the journey that converts (4/5); roll out gradually (3/4). Against: explore radically different directions (6/6) and take aesthetic risks (4/5).** — Explore radically, ship gradually. Radical divergence is a thinking tool. It applies fully to new products and to the visual-voice layer. For a live product with users and flows that convert, ship through the low-risk lever order (type, spacing, colour, motion, hero, blocks). Keep URLs, nav labels and placements, and roll out behind flags and betas with a revert option. Ship a radical change only when the old form blocks a new capability and the new version measurably beats it.
- **Judge a page by whether it solves the business and user problem, not by how clean or modern it looks (4/5). Against: treat beauty as core from the start (5/5); quality and aesthetics are the growth engine (3/3); finish what nobody checks (4/4).** — Quality is judged on both counts, and the outcome test wins any direct clash on a specific decision. Never trade a converting flow or clarity for looks; measure before and after, and ship visual changes to revenue-critical pages as A/B tests or staged rollouts. Craft investment wins wherever it doesn't hurt outcomes (states, edges, emails, details), because perceived quality builds trust over time (the aesthetic-usability effect). Check assumptions with real users (process_mindset.0.44) rather than peer approval.
- **Generate every size from one modular ratio of about 1.25 and delete off-scale sizes (7/7). Against: at least one jump of 3x or more, since a scale with every step under 1.25 reads flat (3/3); headlines at least 2x body with dramatic jumps (3/3); oversized display near viewport width (5/5). Apple's own ladder (34/28/22/20/17/15/13/12/11) is fixed and named but not geometric (8/9, 7/7).** — Use one named ladder (typography.0.36), but it need not follow a single geometric ratio. Text and UI steps stay tight: 1.125-1.2, or the platform ladder exactly (native Apple apps use the system text styles). Display steps jump: headline at least 2x body, and one display size at least 3x body per marketing view. Implement display sizes as fluid clamp() tokens with a cap (about 6-8rem), going near viewport width only for a single wordmark moment. Dense dashboards don't need the 3x jump and can build hierarchy from weight and colour. Every size, including display, is a token; nothing is a one-off.
- **Skip at least one weight step and never rely on 400 vs 600, ideally 100-200 against 800-900 (3/3). Against: Regular 400 plus SemiBold 600 (4/4); a same-size Semibold Headline over Regular Body is Apple's method (3/3); no Thin, Light or Black at text sizes (3/3).** — 400/600 is the default because in practice it comes with a size or colour difference too. When weight is the ONLY difference at the same size and the face's semibold is subtle, step up to 400/700. Extreme pairs (thin against black) belong only to display sizes above about 32px, consistent with typography.0.39. Use 2 weights per screen and 2-4 per product.
- **Never eyeball: take every value from one 8-based scale and snap to the grid (11/11); use systems, not dragging (4/4); no one-off text styles (8/9); use a 12/8/4-column grid (3/4). Against: space optically, not by bounding box, by trimming line-box excess, centring icons optically and lining up stems (4/4); zoom out and add weight wherever text is hard to read (4/4); place one key element deliberately off the grid (3/3); principles are defaults, not dogma (6/7).** — Tokens and grid win by default; the evidence is strong. Optical corrections are allowed, but systematised. Build them into components once: cap-height trimming in buttons and pills (text-box-trim), optical offsets inside the icon component, a token for hanging punctuation. Don't apply them ad hoc on each page. A grid break is allowed once per view, as a deliberate isolation of the key element (hierarchy.0.19), with everything else aligned. If you need an off-scale value twice, add a token; if only once, the value is probably wrong.
- **Use one small radius scale and never mix radii (5/5). Against: one radius everywhere is a tell, so vary radius by hierarchy (4/4), and concentric nesting requires different radii (4/4). Also: sharp corners for luxury, rounded only for friendly tones (3/3), versus continuous-curve squircles as the default shape (3/3).** — Use one documented radius scale with steps by role, for example 8 for inputs, 12-16 for cards, 20-28 for large containers, and full pills only for actions. Nest radii concentrically (inner = outer minus padding). That satisfies both 'one scale' and 'vary by hierarchy'. Sharp versus soft is a brand decision: 0-4px for luxury, editorial or professional brands, soft for consumer tech. Never mix the two in one view. Continuous squircle corners are the default on native Apple (.continuous) and in Figma (corner smoothing). On the web, use them where corner-shape is supported or via a mask on large hero containers; otherwise a plain radius is fine.
- **Build two-layer shadows with sm/md/lg elevation levels (3/3); soft, diffuse shadows, one source tinting them with the brand hue (3/3). Against: show elevation with tonal surfaces and keep shadows for floating controls (3/3); the same rgba shadow everywhere is a tell (4/4); no coloured glows (3/3). Colour: tint shadows toward a coloured background (3/3) versus keep them neutral (3/3).** — Tonal surfaces carry in-flow elevation by default. Layered two-part shadows (the components_ui.0.52 recipe) go only on things that actually float: menus, popovers, dialogs, sticky bars, dragged items. Product cut-outs get only a soft contact shadow. Colour: neutral on neutral grounds; on a coloured ground, a darker, low-chroma shade of that ground (color.0.32). Never saturated brand-hue glows or zero-offset halos.
- **Separate with whitespace, and draw the remaining borders at low contrast (5/5); no full-contrast borders on cards or chips (3/3). Against: require 3:1 for non-text UI, including card outlines, controls and inactive nav (8/10); buttons must look pressable, for example with a 1px border (4/4).** — WCAG 1.4.11 requires 3:1 only for boundaries needed to identify a component or its state, and that settles this. Decorative separators and card edges can be faint or absent when spacing or surface already does the job. Inputs, checkboxes, toggles, outline buttons, focus rings and selected states must meet 3:1, and so must any card whose outline is the only sign it is interactive. For a quiet input, use a tonal fill with a 3:1 edge or focus state rather than a faint hairline.
- **Body text needs at least 4.5:1, aiming for 7:1 (8/10); keep strong contrast in both themes (5/5). Against: secondary text at 40-70% opacity (3/3); muted supporting text at about 65-70% (5/6); low contrast is acceptable for secondary items (3/3).** — Muting is allowed down to the AA floor, never below it. Secondary text must stay at 4.5:1 or better: #6e6e73 on white is about 5:1, and black at about 60% opacity on white is about 5.7:1. Text at 40% opacity is about 2.8:1 and fails; keep that only for states WCAG exempts, such as disabled. Check muted tiers in both themes and on tinted surfaces. Low contrast is fine only for decorative structural lines.
- **Put the single spot colour on the element the eye should land on, for example a key word in the headline (5/5). Against: don't accent one headline word with serif italic, a second colour, gradient text or a highlight (3/3).** — Spot colour goes on the focal object or action (the product, the CTA, the selected state, one data point), not on a headline word. A coloured, italic or gradient word is now a strong AI signature on the web. Exception: poster or campaign art where the word is itself the subject (a product name, a colourway). Use it once per site, in a solid palette colour, never as gradient text.
- **Make the primary CTA a saturated brand colour (3/4), versus use a black primary button in greyscale chrome (7/8), versus a black pill CTA in a grey layout is the trend-minimalism template (3/3). Also: pair a solid primary with a ghost secondary side by side (3/3), versus one CTA per view (4/4) and 'pill CTA plus ghost Watch demo' is the template hero (4/4).** — One primary per view, and it is the highest-contrast clickable element (hierarchy.0.14). COLOUR: the brand accent if the brand has a real one (an Apple-blue-style action colour). Near-black when imagery or product supplies the colour (fashion, luxury, photography-led). A black pill is a choice, not the trend default; on an otherwise grey, minimal page it reads as a template. Never violet. SECONDARY: a text link with a chevron by default. A filled-plus-outline pair only when both are real, specific, parallel actions (Apple's 'Learn more' and 'Buy'), never a vague 'Watch demo'.
- **Keep each decision point to 4 or fewer visible options and top-level nav to 5 or fewer items (4/4); move secondary options into menus (3/3). Against: don't hide functions in overflow just to look minimal (4/4); use a flat top nav of about 7 categories plus 3 icons, no dropdowns, every key destination visible (4/4). Also: hamburger drawer on mobile (8/8).** — Base visibility on frequency and how much the choice matters. Actions people use often, and the destinations they came for, stay visible; rare and advanced ones go behind disclosure. The '4 or fewer' rule applies to a single decision point (a dialog, a CTA group), not the whole nav. Nav count follows the real catalogue. Single-product or service site: 5 or fewer. Multi-category catalogue: about 7-9 flat links, as Apple does, with flyouts only when categories have many children. Mobile web: a drawer is fine. Native apps: a tab bar for 3-5 top-level destinations, per HIG, never a hamburger.
- **Shoot products as isolated cut-outs on a seamless sweep, not in lifestyle scenes (7/7). Against: real photography including lifestyle, at least 2-3 images (5/5); full-bleed scene heroes (4/4); editorial campaign photography (6/6). Sourcing: 'photo-real generated imagery' is acceptable (5/5), versus no generic generated graphics (3/3) and commissioned shoots (6/6).** — Assign images by role. Product, catalogue and detail views: consistent isolated cut-outs on a sweep that matches the section surface. Hero and campaign moments: one art-directed scene per section (product in use, the place, real people). Grade every image on a page with one treatment (imagery_media.0.25). Generated imagery is acceptable only for atmosphere or as comps before a shoot. Never use it to depict the actual product, real customers, results or the team, and replace it with owned photography before a premium launch. For software, real UI screenshots are the product photography (imagery_media.0.30).
- **Add small icons to metadata, labels and tabs so they scan (3/3). Against: use fewer, smaller icons (3/3); cut icon rows (8/9); no icon tiles (7/7). On sourcing: take every icon from one established library and never hand-draw SVG paths (3/4), versus ideally an original, on-brand icon set (6/6), with Lucide called the AI default by one source.** — Use icons where they replace or clarify a word in repeated, scannable data (spec rows, metadata, object types, status, tab bars), at 16-20px. Don't use them as decoration on headings, feature tiles or marketing bullets. SOURCE: SF Symbols on Apple platforms. On the web, one quality established library at a single stroke weight matched to the type, restyled rather than left as the stock default (avoid unmodified Lucide-in-shadcn as the brand's icon language). A commissioned set drawn to one spec is the premium option for flagship brands. 'Never hand-drawn' means never mixing ad-hoc one-off paths into a library set.
- **Break up long pages by giving sections their own background: dark, brand colour or photo (5/5). Against: one design language everywhere (7/7); group with whitespace first (5/5); a neutral palette with one accent (9/9).** — Alternate within the token set: 2-3 surface tokens (white, off-white or light grey, one inverted dark), plus the brand colour or a full-bleed photo for at most one statement section. Never a new hue per section. A background change should mark a change of topic or audience, not decoration.
- **The hero headline should say what the product is, who it's for and the outcome, specifically enough that no competitor could reuse it, in up to 2-3 lines (7/7). Against: headlines of 8 words or fewer, as Apple shows few words (3/3); and the short fragment cadence Apple is known for ('Not X. Just Y.') is now flagged as AI copy (4/4).** — For an unknown brand or product, specificity wins: the headline, or the headline plus one line, must say what it is and what it achieves. Short evocative taglines are only for products whose category the visitor already knows (an established brand, the product named in the nav or an eyebrow). Even then, pair the tagline with a plain descriptive subline. Keep it to 2 lines or fewer on desktop by being specific, not by fragmenting. Avoid formula triads, 'Not X. Just Y.' and em-dash punchlines.
- **Use one identical design language on every touchpoint and at every scale (7/7). Against: share one identity but tune each platform's variant and keep its native conventions (4/4).** — Keep the identity the same everywhere: palette, type voice, imagery treatment, tone of voice, motion character. Keep components and interaction native to each platform: system controls, navigation patterns, text styles and gestures on iOS/macOS; web conventions on the web. 'Identical' applies to the brand layer, 'native' to the behaviour layer.
- **At least 24x24 on desktop and 44x44 on touch (4/4), versus 44x44 CSS px on the web as the minimum everywhere (4/4).** — 44x44 for any coarse pointer (touch, or @media (pointer: coarse)) and for every primary action. 24x24, the WCAG 2.2 AA floor, is allowed only in dense fine-pointer desktop UI such as toolbars and table row actions. Even then, pad the hit area beyond the visible icon and keep enough spacing that targets don't overlap.
- **Web body text at 16-17px and never below 15-16px (7/7), versus compact directories and dashboards with scaled-down type and a 1.125-1.2 scale (3/3, 7/7).** — Reading text stays at 16-17px on the web, or the platform default in apps (macOS 13pt is the native exception). Dense UIs may shrink secondary and data text to 13-15px and tighten the scale. The primary data point and any running text stay at 15px or above, and nothing goes below the platform minimum (11pt on iOS, about 12px on the web).
- **Reserve red and green for error and success (4/4). Against: reuse the brand's own bold accent even when it is red (3/3); choose the hue for its meaning in the domain (3/3). Also: keep bright red and yellow out of dominant roles for premium (4/4).** — Semantic meaning wins. If the brand accent is red or green, it can stay the accent, but status must be distinguishable from it. Errors use a different shade or hue plus an icon and text; success uses a non-brand green. The accent never sits on destructive actions unless they really are destructive. For premium work, a red brand accent stays small (logo, CTA), never a dominant ground.
- **Big hero headlines at a line-height of about 0.9 (3/3), versus display around 1.0 and headings 1.1-1.3 (6/6), and never tight leading on 3 or more lines (3/3).** — Default display leading is 1.0-1.1. Go to 0.9-0.95 only for 1-2 line headlines in all-caps or a condensed face, where descenders and ascenders won't collide. Anything that wraps to 3 or more lines gets 1.05 or more. Body stays at about 1.5.

Require at least 4.5:1 for body and small text, 3:1 for large text, and 3:1 for non-text UI (icons, controls, focus rings, card outlines, inactive nav items, notification badges). Aim for 7:1 for body text on dark surfaces. Never use pale grey for body or dialog text, and never recolor status badges in the brand hue if that drops them below 3:1. | accessibility_performance | **8** / 10 (6v/4a) | 5 | Consensus: 4.5:1 for normal text and 3:1 for large text (vbFn0C-pvis#16, a04#19, a09#17); 3:1 for controls, icons and focus indicators (a04#19); a 7:1 (AAA) target for body text on dark surfaces (a06#6). | 1 | ⛔ Don't assemble the page from a template or from your category's stock section sequence. ### components_ui (60)

| # | Decision | Theme | Creators / sources (videos/articles) | Research sets | Specifics |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | ✅ Follow established conventions and platform standards (Jakob's law) instead of inventing new interaction models. Put the logo top-left, nav at the top or side, a prominent header search and account/cart top-right, and use a hamburger drawer on mobile and standard submenus, filters, modals, carts and checkout. Label any non-standard element explicitly. | components_ui | **8** / 8 (4v/4a) | 5 | Amazon and Wikipedia headers. Follow the Apple HIG for window chrome and navigation so an Electron app feels native. Use a magnifier for search and a cart for checkout. |
| 2 | ✅ Build UIs from a coded, modular component library of flexible, composable components and complete blocks, with states and motion built in. Design with the library code rather than hand-rolling or drawing static mockups. | components_ui | **5** / 5 (2v/3a) | 4 | React + Tailwind + shadcn/ui; reui.io, kibo-ui, heroui and Relume blocks (forms, grids, kanban, auth, wizards). 50+ fully flexible components. Intercom designs with pattern-library code instead of Photoshop. |
| 3 | ✅ Pick one small corner-radius scale and use it on every component and section: all sharp (0-4px), all soft (12-16px), or a documented mix by role. Never mix rounded and sharp corners or improvised in-between radii in one view. If you use full pills, reserve them for actions (CTAs, chips, search). | components_ui | **5** / 5 (2v/3a) | 3 | Documented mix: pill buttons, 16px cards, 8px inputs. | 4 | ✅ Use translucent frosted glass (backdrop blur) only on the functional layer that floats above content: sticky sub-navs, toolbars, tab bars, sidebars and overlays. There it replaces a shadow. | 8 | ✅ Make hit targets large (Fitts's law): at least 24x24px on desktop and 44x44px on touch, with at least 0.4cm between adjacent touch targets. Make frequently used targets bigger and put them near the pointer or thumb. Pad small icons so the clickable area matches or exceeds the visual, with no dead zones. | components_ui | **4** / 4 (1v/3a) | 3 | 24px desktop / 44px mobile minimum (a14). | 9 | ✅ Make an element's look match its behaviour: buttons must look pressable (native button, or a fill, 1px border, ~0.5rem radius, hover colour change and pointer cursor). Style only clickable things as buttons, give identical-looking controls identical behaviour, and avoid ambiguous lines or unlabelled icons that might or might not be interactive. | components_ui | **4** / 4 (3v/1a) | 3 | An unstyled <input type="submit"> renders as an OS button. | 10 | ✅ Group closely related tabs and controls inside one shared container (pill track or unified toolbar group), merge similar actions, and set unrelated items apart. | 2 | ✅ Treat any AI output as a draft that is about 80% done, and never accept a one-shot result as finished. About 15 minutes of tweaks (remove emojis, borders and purple, resize icons, adjust gaps) turned 'cheap and generic' into polished. 'There's no easy button for good work.' |
| 3 | ✅ Work top-down in ordered layers. Start with the outcome (what the product makes easier), then structure (a greyscale, black-on-white wireframe with real content, signed off), then navigation, interaction and content, and add visual styling last, each style only to solve a named problem. Change one layer per pass and check after each that the whole still holds. | process_mindset | **7** / 7 (4v/3a) | 5 | Outcome → Structure → Interaction → Visual, adapted from Garrett's Elements of UX. ### spacing_layout (45)

| # | Decision | Theme | Creators / sources (videos/articles) | Research sets | Specifics |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | ✅ Use far more whitespace than feels necessary. Roughly double your default margins and gaps (2-3x a standard interface for premium or editorial layouts) and add extra vertical space on mobile. Leave empty areas empty: never enlarge text, add background layers or pad the page out to fill a gap. | spacing_layout | **16** / 17 (12v/5a) | 6 | 'Double your whitespace', 'sometimes a ridiculous amount' (a12#20). 'Double your margins. Then double them again' (a30#16). On Apple pages a whole viewport can hold one headline or one product shot (cdzbM0HABqY#0). Flyer |
| 2 | ✅ Set every gap by relationship, never one uniform value. Keep related items tight (4-16px, with labels 4-8px from their input) and separate groups by at least 2x the inner gap (24-80px). Put the largest gaps between sections: about 32px on mobile app screens and 80-96px on desktop marketing pages. Each level up the hierarchy (lines < elements < groups < sections) gets strictly more space, and no item may sit equidistant between two groups. | spacing_layout | **14** / 17 (10v/7a) | 5 | Within groups 8/16px and between groups 64/80px (a09#25). Labels 4-8px from inputs, heading-to-content vs heading-to-previous gap about 1:2-1:3 (soZgjJi7xNw#20). Cards 8/16px apart, heading 24px from its group, 32px betw |
| 3 | ✅ Take every spacing and sizing value from one fixed scale stored as named tokens (CSS custom properties or Sass/LESS variables). Never eyeball a gap or use a one-off pixel value. The consensus scale is 8-based with 4 as a half-step: 4, 8, 12, 16, 24, 32, 48, 64, 96, 128px. In CSS, set a 16px root and step in 0.5rem. Snap elements to the 8px grid, and on widths not divisible by 8 keep padding on the scale and let the block stretch. | spacing_layout | **11** / 11 (7v/4a) | 7 | Scale 4-128px, with controls sized from it: 40px buttons, 32px pills, 16/24px icons (soZgjJi7xNw#19). | 4 | ✅ Align every element to at least one other element along shared horizontal and vertical axes (a grid line, edge, baseline or optical centre). Check by drawing guide lines along the edges. | spacing_layout | **7** / 7 (4v/3a) | 4 | On The Ordinary ad, the logo, tagline, headline, body and icon list share a left edge (MUc3J6mAvFI#6). | 5 | ✅ Bind each heading to the content it introduces. | 1 | ✅ Track inversely to size. Give small labels, captions and uppercase eyebrows +0.03 to +0.1em. Leave web body text at or near 0. Tighten headings step by step from about -0.005em at h6 to -0.02em at h1, and go no tighter than about -0.04em even on the largest display type. Never use negative tracking at 12px or smaller. With the system font, rely on its built-in size-specific tracking, and apply its tracking table by hand only in static mockups. | typography | **11** / 11 (4v/7a) | 7 | h6 -0.5%, h3 -1.5%, h1 -2%. Labels: 12px caps at +0.06em or +0.1em; a 13px eyebrow at +0.4px. Display: Linear -3px at 80px (about -0.04em), Stripe -1.4px at 56px, Apple -0.28px at 56px. SF Pro table in 1/1000 em: 10pt +1 |
| 2 | ✅ Don't ship the template or default sans. Choose a distinctive, still-uncommon typeface for this project whose category matches the brand voice: monospace for technical, editorial serif for credibility and tradition, a characterful grotesk for startup. Audition at least 3 candidates in the real layout and let the headline type carry the brand. Never use a system display face (Impact, Arial Black, the platform sans) as the display voice. | typography | **10** / 13 (6v/7a) | 5 | Code: JetBrains Mono, Fira Code, Space Grotesk. | 3 | ✅ Set the whole interface in one clean, highly readable sans family: the system font (SF Pro or system-ui) or Inter, DM Sans, Plus Jakarta Sans or Geist. Never pair two faces of the same class (sans + sans, serif + serif, slab + slab, script + script) or near-twins. | 27 | ✅ Use real typographic characters, not typewriter approximations. ## Evidence: which sources back the top 60

1. **Use far more whitespace than feels necessary. Roughly double your default margins and gaps (2-3x a standard interface for premium or editorial layouts) and add extra vertical space on mobile. Leave empty areas empty: never enlarge text, add background layers or pad the page out to fill a gap.** — NaughtyyJuan: The Ultimate Apple Style Animation Guide; Kole Jain: 7 UI/UX mistakes that SCREAM youre a beginner; Kole Jain: Every UI/UX Concept Explained in Under 10 Minutes; Ana Denis | Expat Career Reinvention: 6 INSANE Website Design Tricks Luxury Brands Use | How to Give your Website a Premium Look; TemplateMonster: SECRETS to Apple Landing Page Design // How to Design Cool Interactive; Grafisite: Why Doesn't Your Design Look Premium? 2. **Set every gap by relationship, never one uniform value. Keep related items tight (4-16px, with labels 4-8px from their input) and separate groups by at least 2x the inner gap (24-80px). Put the largest gaps between sections: about 32px on mobile app screens and 80-96px on desktop marketing pages. 3. **Don't assemble the page from a template or from your category's stock section sequence. 5. **Track inversely to size. Give small labels, captions and uppercase eyebrows +0.03 to +0.1em. Leave web body text at or near 0. 8. **Take every spacing and sizing value from one fixed scale stored as named tokens (CSS custom properties or Sass/LESS variables). Never eyeball a gap or use a one-off pixel value. 21. **When you use two typefaces, give headings and body clearly different faces from different classifications that still share a mood. 26. **Generate every font size from one modular scale instead of eyeballing. Start from a 16px (1rem) body size and multiply by a fixed ratio of about 1.25 (Major Third; 1.27 is the square root of the golden ratio), or 1.125-1.2 for dense dashboards and mobile UIs. Snap every size to the scale and delete off-scale or fractional sizes. Keep the full golden ratio (1.618) for a single headline-to-body jump, because across 4 or more levels it reaches 110px.** — Grafisite: Why Doesn't Your Design Look Premium? Then narrow to a few directions and cherry-pick the best details.** — Aliena Cai: How I use (and dont use) AI to design better in 2026; Tom Is Loading: 10 Ways to Prevent AI Slop in Your Frontend UIs; Self-Made Web Designer: 6 EASY Tips to 10x Any Site's Design; Flux Academy: Design The Perfect Hero Section (With Example); Kyle Chayka: The A.I.-Design Aesthetic That's Taking Over the Internet; Karri Saarinen, Yann-Edern Gillet, Andreas Eldh, Romain Cascino (Linear): How we redesigned the Linear UI (part II)
48. **Make the real product the star of each section. Show actual UI screenshots, real code, real generated outputs or real interior pages (static, lightly edited, skewed, or as an animated or interactive mockup) instead of abstract marketing illustrations, glows or orbs.** — VoltAgent; sources read: README.md, CONTRIBUTING.md, design-md/apple/DESIGN.md, design-md/linear.app/DESIGN.md, design-md/vercel/DESIGN.md, design-md/stripe/DESIGN.md, design-md/claude/DESIGN.md, plus a grep pass over all 74 design-md/*/DESIGN.md files for recurring Do/Don't rules: awesome-design-md: Curated collection of DESIGN.md files extracted from real websites; DesignCourse: Go from VIBE DESIGNS to PRO DESIGN - 2 TIPS; Kole Jain: 5 SaaS UI/UX mistakes that SCREAM you Vibe Code; uxpeak: Top 5 UX/UI Design Tips and Tricks Every Designer Needs to Know About; Roman Kamushken: Why every AI startup looks the same (and how to design one that doesn't); Ana Denis | Expat Career Reinvention: 6 INSANE Website Design Tricks Luxury Brands Use | How to Give your Website a Premium Look
49. **Don't ship change that is merely different, such as a new skin or surface material (transparency, metal, wood), or novelty for its own sake. Change form only when it adds a functional property (new interaction, depth, context) or a new technical capability opens it up, and only when the result is clearly better than what it replaces.** — Think Multiply: Apple's Design Philosophy; DW Euromaxx: Ask a Designer: Dieter Rams' 10 principles of good design; Design Lovers: Apple Design: why we keep coming back to this?; Malewicz: UI design is changing. New trends emerge.; Dieter Rams (published by Vitsoe): The power of good design (Dieter Rams's ten principles); Daniel Scrivner (book summary of Jony Ive's 2016 Apple anthology): Designed by Apple in California by Jony Ive
50. **Use few images, and make each one high-resolution, art-directed, editorial-grade photography, commissioned or custom-shot (ideally by one photographer) rather than generic stock. Art-direct lighting, composition and negative space as carefully as the subject. Keep video poster frames sharp and matching the video they open.** — Flux Academy: $500 vs $5,000 vs $50,000 Websites (With Examples); Ana Denis | Expat Career Reinvention: 6 INSANE Website Design Tricks Luxury Brands Use | How to Give your Website a Premium Look; Nevra Aslan: Designing Digital Luxury: How to Design Interfaces That Feel Expensive; Tony Chambers: Sir Jony Ive reflects on the nature of objects, the fragility of ideas, and 20 years of Apple design; Y Combinator: Common Mistakes With Vibe Coded Websites; TemplateMonster: SECRETS to Apple Landing Page Design // How to Design Cool Interactive
51. **Keep every image on a page in one visual style. Grade and retouch all photos with one treatment of tone, light, contrast and framing, and never mix illustration styles (no stray gradient or 3D asset in a flat set). Match the imagery to the UI: clay illustrations for a clay UI, soft studio photos for a soft UI. Re-shoot or drop outliers instead of mixing quality levels.** — Ana Denis | Expat Career Reinvention: 6 INSANE Website Design Tricks Luxury Brands Use | How to Give your Website a Premium Look; Dgrees: Spain Collection: Evolving a Luxury Website into a Digital Ecosystem; Tony Chambers: Sir Jony Ive reflects on the nature of objects, the fragility of ideas, and 20 years of Apple design; Code Munchies: 5 UI design trends that don't SUCK in 2025; Satori Graphics: 6 Golden Rules Of Layout Design You MUST OBEY; Grafisite: Why Doesn't Your Design Look Premium? Fix These 7 Things
52. **Keep navigation and chrome visually subordinate to the workspace. Dim the sidebar a few notches, shrink headers, greetings and page titles, and let the content (charts, data, lists) be the focal point.** — Jesse Showalter: Amateur vs Pro UI Design | with examples; Karri Saarinen, Yann-Edern Gillet, Andreas Eldh, Romain Cascino (Linear): How we redesigned the Linear UI (part II); Charlie Aufmann, Maxime Heckel (Linear): A calmer interface for a product in motion; Apple Newsroom (press release, June 9, 2025; quotes Alan Dye, VP of Human Interface Design): Apple introduces a delightful and elegant new software design; Alice Milo: Liquid Glass: Redefining design through Hierarchy, Harmony and Consistency; Raluca Budiu: Liquid Glass Is Cracked, and Usability Suffers in iOS 26
53. **Train your eye on real work every day. Keep a personal inspiration library of elements, videos and images you like, reverse-engineer admired designs by measuring their values and asking what the designer left out and focused on, critique and quickly redesign weak screens, and recreate interactions until you can say exactly why they feel right.** — Oliur Online: Stop AI slop and making stuff look vibe coded; Somrat Dutta: Give me 4.23 Minutes and I'll improve your DESIGN by 160%; Malewicz: The famous Apple design ...I just had to redesign it! (Penpot); Oliur: How to make AI designs look less like AI; Erik D. Kennedy: 7 Rules for Creating Gorgeous UI (Part 1, updated for 2024); Rauno Freiberg: Invisible Details of Interaction Design
54. **Set titles and key values bold at full contrast, and their supporting text smaller, in Regular weight and a muted shade (about 65-70% of text contrast). Never let supporting text outweigh its title, and use the same style for every repeated pair.** — DesignWithArash: UI Design Principles | Everything You Need To Know; Malewicz: The famous Apple design ...I just had to redesign it! (Penpot); Sajid: The Biggest Lie in UI Design; Sajid: The Easy Way to Pick UI Colors; Kelley Gordon (Nielsen Norman Group, Jan 17, 2021): Visual Hierarchy in UX: Definition; NaughtyyJuan: The Ultimate Apple Style Animation Guide
55. **Give monospace (SF Mono, ui-monospace, Geist Mono, JetBrains Mono) or a technical grotesque one narrow job: code, terminal UI, IDs and status tokens, timestamps, data, image captions, small technical eyebrows, and anything that must line up in character cells. Never set body paragraphs or marketing copy in it. If it is a third face, this is its only role.** — VoltAgent; sources read: README.md, CONTRIBUTING.md, design-md/apple/DESIGN.md, design-md/linear.app/DESIGN.md, design-md/vercel/DESIGN.md, design-md/stripe/DESIGN.md, design-md/claude/DESIGN.md, plus a grep pass over all 74 design-md/*/DESIGN.md files for recurring Do/Don't rules: awesome-design-md: Curated collection of DESIGN.md files extracted from real websites; Matthew Butterick: Summary of key rules (Butterick's Practical Typography); Blake Crosley (May 02, 2026): SF Pro: Variable Axes, Optical Sizing, And The Dynamic Type Contract; Kole Jain: Mathematically Perfect Typography for Web Design; Google Labs (google-labs-code); sources read: README.md, PHILOSOPHY.md, docs/spec.md, examples/*/DESIGN.md: DESIGN.md: open specification for describing a design system to coding agents
56. **Budget deliberate effort on every project to steer away from the AI tool's default house style. Before building, review the plan for any part you would produce for any similar prompt, revise it, and note what changed. Before shipping, scan the page with a fixed checklist or deterministic DOM/computed-style scan for template and AI tells, and keep fixing until it has 0-1 default patterns (3 or fewer on a 10-item 'are you a template?' audit). Never ship the default look with only your logo swapped in.** — Adrian Krebs: Scoring Show HN submissions for AI design patterns; Kyle Chayka: The A.I.-Design Aesthetic That's Taking Over the Internet; Leonxlnx (Leon), with contributors: Taste Skill: The Anti-Slop Frontend Framework for AI Agents; Roman Kamushken: Why every AI startup looks the same (and how to design one that doesn't); Anthropic: Frontend Design (frontend-design SKILL.md)
57. **Match optical size to point size: use the Text cut below 20pt and the Display cut at 20pt and above, or a variable font's opsz axis. On a 1x/72dpi canvas, switch at 40pt. Prefer the system font, or fonts with optical sizing, so the cut and per-size tracking change automatically. Avoid custom fonts without optical sizing in UIs that span many sizes. When you need a second style, take it from a superfamily or companion family designed to work together.** — Self-Made Web Designer: 6 EASY Tips to 10x Any Site's Design; Karri Saarinen, Yann-Edern Gillet, Andreas Eldh, Romain Cascino (Linear): How we redesigned the Linear UI (part II); Apple (Human Interface Guidelines): Typography - Human Interface Guidelines; Nick Keppol: Arriving at San Francisco; Blake Crosley (May 02, 2026): SF Pro: Variable Axes, Optical Sizing, And The Dynamic Type Contract
58. **Never hard-code font sizes. Define typography as named text-style tokens (title, headline, body, caption) in units that follow the user's text-size setting: rem on the web, Dynamic Type text styles on iOS. Give custom and brand fonts the same scaling and Bold Text support; in SwiftUI always pass relativeTo:, for example .custom("Brand", size: 16, relativeTo: .body). Derive bold, italic and tracking variants from the base style.** — Sajid: The 80% of UI Design - Typography; Nick Keppol: Arriving at San Francisco; Jason Zhou: Apple Design System Breakdown, With a Copyable DESIGN.md (2026); Apple (Human Interface Guidelines): Typography - Human Interface Guidelines; Blake Crosley (May 02, 2026): SF Pro: Variable Axes, Optical Sizing, And The Dynamic Type Contract
59. **Make every property a deliberate, explainable decision and leave nothing at its default. Pick any element at random and be able to justify its size, spacing, alignment, colour, shadow and animation duration.** — BehindTheScreen: The SECRET Trick to Premium Website Design; Anthony Hobday: Visual design rules you can safely follow every time; Dieter Rams (published by Vitsoe): The power of good design (Dieter Rams's ten principles); Tim Brown (A List Apart, May 3, 2011): More Meaningful Typography; Nevra Aslan: Designing Digital Luxury: How to Design Interfaces That Feel Expensive
60. **Budget visual beauty as a core part of the product from the start, not a finishing layer. Design form and function together so every aesthetic choice supports the content and task, and frame goals around 'quality' so they cover both looks and function.** — Kate Moran: The Aesthetic-Usability Effect; Dieter Rams (published by Vitsoe): The power of good design (Dieter Rams's ten principles); Figma Blog (panel: Katie Dill of Stripe, Karri Saarinen of Linear, Yuhki Yamashita of Figma), May 9, 2024: Craft and Beauty: The ROI of Marrying Form and Function; Nevra Aslan: Designing Digital Luxury: How to Design Interfaces That Feel Expensive; Rauno Freiberg: Invisible Details of Interaction Design
